"""Parallel program optimization: measured on real processes, plus a scheduler
simulator driven by measured task times.

Real measurements use `multiprocessing` (separate processes, so Python's GIL does
not serialise them) with BLAS/OpenMP threads pinned to 1 so NumPy cannot secretly
use extra cores. On a machine with C cores, speed-up cannot exceed C; running
more processes than cores (oversubscription) is measured on purpose.

Concepts and where they are shown
  speed-up S(p) = T(1)/T(p), efficiency E(p) = S(p)/p          strong_scaling
  Amdahl:     S(p) = 1 / ((1 - f) + f / p)   (fixed problem; f = parallel fraction)
  Gustafson:  S(p) = (1 - f) + f p           (problem grows with p)   weak_scaling
  load balancing, makespan                                       load_balance, simulate

import pytest

from distopt import learning as L, parallel as P, submodular as S

def test_diminishing_returns_exhaustive_on_small_coverage():
    """Every (A ⊆ B, e) triple on a 7-set instance, not just random ones."""
    obj = S.Coverage(n_sets=7, n_points=60, seed=3)
    for r in range(7):
        for B in itertools.combinations(range(7), r):
            for a in range(r + 1):
                for A in itertools.combinations(B, a):

                        ga = obj.f(set(A) | {e}) - obj.f(set(A))
                        gb = obj.f(set(B) | {e}) - obj.f(set(B))

Three parts, each with its own baseline and correctness check:

| Parallel (`distopt/parallel.py`) | minimise wall-clock time / makespan | the serial program; static block scheduling; the ILP-optimal makespan | every parallel result equals the serial result (sums, gradients to 4e-14, layer outputs exactly); simulator checked by hand calculation and Graham's LPT bound |
| Distributed / multi-task (`distopt/learning.py`) | train a model across clients; train two tasks on a shared layer | centralized SGD with the same epochs; equal task weights | gradients vs finite differences; FedAvg with one client = centralized SGD exactly; MGDA's closed form vs a grid search |

22 tests (`tests/test_distopt.py`). **The machine has only 2 CPU cores**, so

rerun on a laptop with more cores.

### **Claude** · 2026-10-31 03:08:25

Track D is committed and all 22 of its tests pass. The updated repo is in optimize-anything.zip. Nine of the ten tracks are done; only J is left.

- **Submodular:**
  - **The check works:** it found no diminishing-returns violations on coverage and facility location. It found 2,419 on the supermodular "synergy" function and 970 on feature-selection R², out of 3,000 trials each.
  - **Coverage:** greedy matched the exact optimum for every k from 2 to 8. Lazy greedy got the same sets with far fewer evaluations (71 vs 292 at k = 8).
  - **R² (not submodular):** greedy fell below the 63% guarantee, reaching 50% of the optimum.
- **Parallel speed-up (this machine has only 2 cores):** 1.72× with no serial code and 1.41× with 30% serial code. Both are below what Amdahl's law predicts, because starting processes and uneven work cost time the formula ignores. Weak scaling (a problem that grows with the process count) reached 1.71×.
- **Load balancing:**
  - **Real runs:** splitting the tasks into contiguous blocks took 0.83 s; handing them out round-robin or from a shared queue took 0.59 s.
  - **Simulation on 16 workers:** work stealing stayed within 18% of the optimal schedule; longest-task-first was within 4%.
- **Overheads:**
  - **Tiny tasks:** sending them one per message took 4.7 s, against 0.03 s in chunks of 10,000.
  - **Locks and memory access:** a lock on every counter increment was 21× slower than counting locally. Reading a matrix by columns was 6.8× slower than by rows.
  - **Data movement:** keeping data in the workers instead of sending it every step took data-parallel training from 8.8 s to 0.36 s.
- **FedAvg:**
  - **Clients with mixed data:** many local epochs work almost for free (0.948 accuracy with 10× less communication).
  - **Clients that each hold about 2 digits:** with the same amount of training, 10 local epochs reached 0.837 against 0.922 for 1 epoch, because each client's model drifts towards its own digits.
- **Multi-task:**
  - **Equal weights:** the small task isn't learned at all (error 1.04, no better than predicting the mean).
  - **MGDA:** brings that error down to 0.24, but with normalised gradients it always reduces to a plain average of the two directions.
  - **GradNorm:** fixes the imbalance too, but its learned weights collapse to (2, 0).

I found and fixed two bugs in the generated code, now failure log entries F12 and F13. The multi-task test set had come from a different task, and the GradNorm code contradicted its own comments. Track I's comparison with parallel computing is now filled in, and the root README checklist rows for Track D are complete.

"""Test-suite minimization (weighted set cover) and prioritization.

Formulation (minimization)
  decision variables  x_t ∈ {0, 1}          run test t or not
  objective           min  Σ_t c_t x_t       c_t = measured seconds (or 1 to minimise the count)
  constraints         Σ_{t covers r} x_t ≥ 1   for every requirement r covered by the full suite
Requirements r are statements and/or branch arcs of the library (see collect.py).
This is the (weighted) set-cover problem: NP-hard; greedy is an H(m) ≈ ln m + 1
approximation (Chvátal 1979); the ILP is exact.

Prioritization orders ALL tests so that faults are found early:
  total      : most requirements first
  additional : most not-yet-covered requirements first (reset when everything is covered)
  additional per second : as above, divided by test time
Quality is measured by APFD / cost-aware APFDc on a mutant kill matrix (mutate.py).
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import math
import random

import pulp


def requirements(cov, kinds=("L", "A")):
    return {t: {r for r in rs if r[0] in kinds} for t, rs in cov.items()}


def universe(cov):
    return set().union(*cov.values()) if cov else set()


def is_cover(selected, cov, U=None):
    U = universe(cov) if U is None else U
    got = set().union(*(cov[t] for t in selected)) if selected else set()
    return U <= got


def essential_tests(cov):
    """Tests that are the ONLY test covering some requirement: every cover must contain them."""

    for t, rs i

def _nth_prime_bounds(n):
    """Bounds for the nth prime (counting from 1): lb < p_n < ub."""
    # At and above 688,383, the lb/ub spread is under 0.003 * p_n.

    if n < 1:
        raise ValueError

    if n < 6:
        return (n, 2.25 * n)

    # https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime-counting_function#Inequalities
    upper_bound = n * log(n * log(n))
    lower_bound = upper_bound - n
    if n >= 688_383:
        upper_bound -= n * (1.0 - (log(log(n)) - 2.0) / log(n))

"""Mutation testing: what fault-detection does a reduced suite give up?

A mutant is the library with ONE small change (a seeded bug) on one line. The suite
"kills" a mutant if at least one test fails. We build a kill matrix
    kills[mutant] = set of tests that fail on it
once, and then evaluate any subset or ordering of tests from it without rerunning.

Speed-up that keeps the matrix exact: a test that never executes the mutated line
cannot see the change, so only the tests whose coverage includes that line are run.
Lines executed at import time are excluded (every test would 'cover' them).

Operators (one AST node, single-line nodes only, so line numbers never shift):
  comparison  <  <=  >  >=  ==  !=  is / is not  in / not in   (swapped with its partner)
  arithmetic  + <-> -    * <-> //    // -> *    % -> //
  boolean     and <-> or ;  not x -> x
  constant    integer k -> k + 1 ;  True <-> False
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import ast
import copy
import os
import random
import shutil
import subprocess
from dataclasses import dataclass
from pathlib import Path

from . import collect as C

CMP = {ast.Lt: ast.LtE, ast.LtE: ast.Lt, ast.Gt: ast.GtE, ast.GtE: ast.Gt, ast.Eq: ast.NotEq,
       ast.NotEq: ast.Eq, ast.Is: ast.IsNot, ast.IsNot: ast.Is, ast.In: ast.NotIn, ast.NotIn: ast.In}
BIN = {ast.Add: ast.Sub, ast.Sub: ast.Add, ast.Mult: ast.FloorDiv, ast.FloorDiv: ast.Mult, ast.Mod: ast.FloorDiv}


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Mutant:
    id: int
    file: str

new='''def run_coverage(db: Path, tests=None, subject=SUBJECT):
    cmd = pytest_cmd([f"--cov={PACKAGE}", "--cov-branch", "--cov-context=test", "--cov-report="], tests)
    subprocess.run(cmd, cwd=subject, env=dict(env(subject), COVERAGE_FILE=str(db)), check=True,
                   capture_output=True)


def coverage_of(tests, subject=SUBJECT):
    """Union of requirements covered when ONLY `tests` are run (fresh process), plus the
    per-test map. Used to verify a reduced suite for real, not from the stored matrix."""
    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as td:
        db = Path(td) / "cov"
        run_coverage(db, tests, subject)
        per = read_coverage(db)
    per.pop(None, None)
    return set().union(*per.values()) if per else set(), per
'''
assert old in s; s=s.replace(old,new); open(p,'w').write(s)
EOF

class _concurrent_tee:
    __slots__ = ('iterator', 'link', 'lock')

    def __init__(self, iterable):
        if isinstance(iterable, _concurrent_tee):
            self.iterator = iterable.iterator
            self.link = iterable.link
            self.lock = iterable.lock
        else:
            self.iterator = iter(iterable)
            self.link = [None, None]
            self.lock = Lock()

    def __iter__(self):
        return self

    def __next__(self):
        link = self.link
        if link[1] is None:
            with self.lock:
                if link[1] is None:
                    link[0] = next(self.iterator)
                    link[1] = [None, None]
        value, self.link = link
        return value

"""Correctness checks for Track J. Run from tracks/J_software_engineering: `pytest -q`.

Fast tests use small hand-made coverage maps. Tests marked `subject` need the
more-itertools checkout (./setup.sh) and the measurements in results/."""
import itertools
import math
from pathlib import Path

import pytest

from tsmin import collect as C, mutate as M, select as S

HERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
MEAS = HERE / "results" / "measurements.json"
needs_subject = pytest.mark.skipif(not (C.SUBJECT.exists() and MEAS.exists()),
                                   reason="run ./setup.sh and run_experiments.py first")

COV = {"a": {1, 2, 3}, "b": {3, 4}, "c": {4, 5, 6}, "d": {1, 6}, "e": {2, 5}, "z": set()}
COST = {"a": 3.0, "b": 1.0, "c": 3.0, "d": 1.0, "e": 1.0, "z": 0.1}


def brute(cov, cost):
    U = S.universe(cov)
    tests = sorted(t for t in cov if cov[t])
    best = None
    for k in range(1, len(tests) + 1):
        for sub in itertools.combinations(tests, k):
            if S.is_cover(sub, cov, U):
                c = sum(cost[t] for t in sub) if cost else len(sub)
                if best is None or c < best[0] - 1e-12:
                    best = (c, sub)
    return best


@pytest.mark.parametrize("cost", [None, COST])
def test_ilp_equals_brute_force(cost):
    sel, val, status, _ = S.ilp(COV, cost)
    assert status == "Optimal"
    assert S.is_cover(sel, COV)
    assert val == pytest.approx(brute(COV, cost)[0])


def test_greedy_is_a_cover_within_the_ln_bound

# J · 001 · Test-suite minimization and prioritization on more-itertools

- **Tool / model:** Claude (claude.ai), Claude Opus 5.5
- **Date:** 2026-10-31
- **Kind of work:** formulation, code generation, measurement, mutation testing, documentation

## Original prompt

After Track D, Claude proposed test-suite minimization as Track J. Reply:

```
yes complete it
```

## AI response (summary)

Claude chose more-itertools v11.2.1 as the subject: a public open-source
library with ~7,300 lines of library code and ~9,200 lines of tests. It wrote:

- `setup.sh`, which clones the subject at that tag.
- `tsmin/collect.py`, which measures per-test time (junit XML, 3 runs,
  without coverage) and per-test statement and branch coverage (coverage.py
  dynamic contexts, read from its SQLite file). It keeps only requirements
  that every coverage run hit.
- `tsmin/select.py`, the formulation:
  - x_t ∈ {0,1}; minimise Σ c_t x_t subject to every requirement being covered.
  - Methods: greedy (count and per-second), redundancy removal, an exact ILP
    (HiGHS), an LP lower bound, and a random-order baseline.
  - Prioritization orders (total, additional, additional per second) scored
    with APFD and APFDc.
- `tsmin/mutate.py`, mutation testing:

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Adaptive timeouts, per-test fallback, resumable mutation log</summary>

```
cd /home/claude/optimize-anything/tracks/J_software_engineering && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='tsmin/mutate.py'; s=open(p).read()
a=s.index("def run_mutant(")
b=s.index("def covering_tests(")
new='''def _budget(tests, times):
    """Wall-clock allowance for running `tests` on a mutant: generous (3x + 10 s) relative
    to their measured time, so only a genuinely hung or runaway test exceeds it."""
    return 10 + 3 * sum(times.get(t, 1.0) for t in tests)


def run_mutant(workdir: Path, m: Mutant, tests, times):
    """Return the set of `tests` that fail on mutant m (None if the mutant does not compile).

    A mutant can make a test loop forever, sometimes inside one huge C-level integer
    operation that pytest-timeout's signal cannot interrupt. So the whole run gets a wall
    clock budget; if it is exceeded, every test is rerun on its own with its own budget,
    and a test that exceeds it counts as failing (in CI it would time out = fault detected)."""
    path = workdir / m.file

# Track J — Software-engineering optimization: test-suite minimization

**Subject:** [more-itertools](https://github.com/more-itertools/more-itertools)
v11.2.1 (commit `4aeb433`, MIT licence).
- **Code under test:** a public open-source library, ~7,300 lines of library
  code and ~9,200 lines of tests.
- **Suite size:** 765 test functions, plus ~21,000 subTests.
- **Not committed:** `setup.sh` clones the library; the checkout itself
  isn't in the repo.

## Formulation

Choose which tests to run in CI so that everything the full suite exercises is
still exercised, in the least time.

| | |
|---|---|
| Decision variables | x_t ∈ {0, 1}: run test t or not (765 variables) |
| Objective | min Σ_t c_t x_t, with c_t = the test's measured time + 1 ms overhead (or c_t = 1 to minimise the count) |
| Constraints | Σ_{t covers r} x_t ≥ 1 for every requirement r covered by the full suite |
| Requirements | 1,960 statements + 2,937 branch arcs of the library (coverage.py, per-test dynamic contexts) |

This is **weighted set cover**, an NP-hard problem.

| Method | What it is |
|---|---|
| Baseline 1 | run everything |
| Baseline 2 | random order until every requirement is covered (naive reduction) |
| Greedy | Chvátal's algorithm, an H(m) ≈ ln m approximation: most new requirements, or most per second, plus a redundancy-removal pass |
| Exact | the ILP solved with HiGHS (gap 0) |
| Lower bound | the LP relaxation |

**Prioritization** (order all tests so faults show early) is scored with 
… [7647 more characters truncated]
```

cd /home/claude/optimize-anything && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='docs/failure_log.md'; s=open(p).read()
entry='''## F14 · Track J · "Statement coverage" that contained no statements

- **Tool:** Claude (claude.ai, Claude Opus 5.5), 2026-10-31
- **What happened:** the first coverage collection reported 2,937
  requirements, all branch arcs and **zero statements**. The
  "statement-coverage" minimization would have been solved over an empty
  universe.
- **Cause:** with branch coverage on, coverage.py stores only arcs in its
  SQLite file; executed lines are derived from them. The generated reader
  queried the line table, which was empty.
- **How it was caught:** printing the requirement counts by kind before
  using them.
- **Fix:** derive statements from arc endpoints, as coverage.py does. A test
  now checks that every statement requirement is an endpoint of some arc.

## F15 · Track J · Mutation runs that never finished

- **Tool:** Claude (claude.ai, Claude Opus 5.5), 2026-10-31
- **What happened:** the first mutation-testing run used pytest-timeout
  (30 s per test). After 45 minutes it had finished fewer than 10% of the
  mutants, and one run on a single mutant didn't end within 10 minutes.
- **Cause:** a mutant such as `x * x % n` → `x * x // n` in the Miller–Rabin
  test makes the numbers double in length every iteration. The time goes
  into one huge C-level multiplication, which pytest-timeout's signal can't
  interrupt.
- **Fix:**
  - A wall-clock budget per mutant (3× th
… [471 more characters truncated]

### **Claude** · 2026-10-31 06:59:24

Track J is committed and its 15 tests pass, so all ten tracks (A–J) are now done. The full repo is in optimize-anything.zip.

For Track J I treated choosing which tests to run as a set-cover problem: pick the cheapest set of tests that still exercises every line and branch the full suite does. The subject is more-itertools v11.2.1, an open-source Python library with 765 tests.

- **Where the time goes:** two tests take 73% of the 17 s of test time. One is a 100-thread stress test that also makes runs unpredictable (12–45 s each). Both are the only tests covering some branch, so no reduced suite can drop them.
- **Minimization:**
  - **Fewer tests:** the exact solver (ILP) cuts the suite from 765 to 296 tests, 61% fewer, with no lost lines or branches when rerun for real. Greedy came within 2% of it, and random selection needed 367 tests.
  - **Little time saved:** the reduced suite is only 15% faster, because the time is in the two slow tests it must keep. Measured run times were too noisy to show even that reliably.
- **What reduction costs:** I seeded 1,116 small bugs (mutation testing). The full suite catches 960 of them.
  - **Reduced suites:** they still miss 7–11% of those 960, because a line running doesn't mean its result was checked.
  - **Random subsets of the same size:** they do far worse, catching only 65–72%.
- **The real gain:** a patch shrinks the two slow tests to the smallest inputs that keep all their coverage and still catch the same seeded bugs. A first, smaller size dropped the lock-contention branch the stress test exists for, which the coverage check caught. Results:
  - The full suite runs in a steady 6.1 s instead of a median of 32.7 s.
  - Combined with the reduced suite, it runs in 3.1 s.
  - The trade-off: 10 threads instead of 100 is a weaker test for race conditions.
- **Test ordering:** putting the tests that cover the most new code per second first finds half the seeded bugs in 0.06 s, against 1.9 s in the original order.

I found and fixed two more bugs in the generated code, now failure log entries F14 and F15 (15 in total). The statement coverage first came back empty, and the per-test timeout couldn't stop seeded bugs that sent prime-number code into runaway arithmetic.

Things only you can do before submitting:

1. **Compare two AI tools:** run two tools on `tracks/F_compiler/tool_comparison/PROMPT.md`, check their answers with `check_submission.py`, then fill in `docs/tool_comparison.md`. The brief requires this.
2. **Save chat exports:** export your AI chats into `prompts/*/exports/`, and fill in the commit hashes in each prompt log after pushing.
3. **Push and add collaborators:** push to GitHub and add akhil-gitcoder and akhilkulal4050: `gh repo create optimize-anything --private --source=. --push`, then `gh api -X PUT repos/<you>/optimize-anything/collaborators/<user>` for each.
4. **Track E data:** download the real Carter exam data into `tracks/E_exam_timetabling/data/` and rerun its experiments.
5. **Rerun on your laptop:** rerun Track D's speed-up experiments, since this machine has only 2 cores. Then run `./setup.sh` in Tracks G, H and J, which fetch their external code.
6. **Check what you'll defend:** work through each prompt log's verification checklist.

The `report/` folder is still empty. I can draft the technical report next, with one section per track drawn from the READMEs.

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Save and score the Sonnet submission</summary>

```
cd /home/claude/optimize-anything/tracks/F_compiler/tool_comparison && cat > submission_claude_sonnet.py <<'EOF'
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

COMMUTATIVE = {"add", "mul", "eq", "ne"}
BINARY = {"add", "sub", "mul", "div", "mod", "shl", "lt", "le", "gt", "ge", "eq", "ne"}


def cse(fn: Function) -> bool:
    changed = False

    for block in fn.blocks.values():
        counter = [0]
        var_vn = {}        # variable -> value number currently held
        holders = {}       # value number -> set of variables holding it
        const_vn = {}      # integer constant -> value number
        table = {}         # expression key -> value number

        def fresh():
            counter[0] += 1
            return counter[0]

        def vn_of(x):
            if isinstance(x, str):
                v = var_vn.get(x)
                if v is None:
                    v = fresh()
                    var_vn[x] = v
                    holders.setdefault(v, set()).add(x)
                return v
            v = const_vn.get(x)
            if v is None:
                v = fresh()
                const_vn[x] = v
            return v

        def assign(var, v):
            old = var_vn.get(var)
            if old is not None:
                holders[old].discard(var)

BINARY_OPS = frozenset({"add", "sub", "mul", "div", "mod", "shl",
                        "lt", "le", "gt", "ge", "eq", "ne"})


def cse(fn: Function) -> bool:
    """Eliminate repeated computations inside each block. Replace a repeated
    computation with `copy dst, <earlier result>`. Mutate fn in place and return
    True if anything changed."""
    changed = False
    for block in fn.blocks.values():
        if _cse_block(block):
            changed = True
    return changed


def _cse_block(block: Block) -> bool:
    # Local value numbering over one straight-line block.
    # Every value gets a number; variables record which number they hold.
    # A repeated expression whose earlier result is still held by some
    # variable is replaced by a copy from that variable.
    changed = False
    counter = 0
    var_vn = {}    # variable -> value number it currently holds
    holders = {}   # value number -> {variable: None} for variables holding it now
    table = {}     # expression key -> value number

    def fresh():
        nonlocal counter
        counter += 1
        return counter

    def set_var(name, v):
        old = var_vn.get(name)
        if old == v:
            return
        if old is not None:
            holders[old].pop(name, None)
        var_vn[name] = v
        holders.setdefault(v, {})[nam
… [1959 more characters truncated]

<details><summary>Tool call: WebFetch — </summary>

```
Exact steps to export Claude data (menu path), and format of the export.
```
</details>

<details><summary>Tool result</summary>

```
**Steps:**

1. Go to **Settings > Privacy** on the web app or Claude Desktop. Export isn't available in the iOS or Android apps.
2. Click "Export data."
3. Wait for the email. It's sent to the address on your account and includes a download link.

The download link expires 24 hours after delivery. You must be signed in to use it. If it expires, repeat the export to get a new one.

**Format:** The page doesn't say what file format the export uses or how the data is structured. It only states that the export includes conversation data and your account's user information.
```

All of Track $T was done in one long claude.ai conversation with Claude Opus 5.5
(2026-10-28 – 11-01). That conversation covered every track in order (E, B, C,
I, G, H, F, A, D, J), and the session compacted its history several times.

What is here, and where the rest is:

- \`../001-*.md\`: the prompt log for this track, with the original prompt, a
  summary of the AI response, revisions, the commit and a verification note.
- \`../../_session/session-2026-10-31_to_11-01.md\`: a verbatim export of the
  part of the conversation still stored in the session (end of Track D,
  all of Track J and the final deliverables). It was made with
  \`tools/export_transcript.py\`, with secrets and e-mail addresses redacted.
- **Full conversation:** export it from claude.ai (**Settings → Privacy →
  Export data**; a download link arrives by e-mail and expires after 24 h).
  Put the conversation's file here, after removing anything private.
EOF
done
cat >> prompts/F/exports/README.md <<'EOF'

Also here: `tool-comparison-claude-sonnet-5.5.md` and
`tool-comparison-claude-haiku-5.5.md`. These are the complete fresh-context
transcripts of the two models used in the tool comparison
(`docs/tool_comparison.md`).
EOF
ls prompts/F/exports

## Results

Generated tables and plots: `results/SUMMARY.md`. Main findings:

1. **Greedy is close but rarely optimal.** Its gap shrinks as n grows (about
   0.3% at 50 items, under 0.02% at 800 for uncorrelated data) but is ~10×
   larger on strongly correlated instances (2.9% at 50 items).
2. **Instance class matters more than size for B&B.** It solves 800
   uncorrelated items in ~0.02 s, but on strongly correlated items it hits the
   5-million-node limit from n = 100. It usually *finds* the optimum early but
   cannot *prove* it: all item ratios are nearly equal, so the fractional
   bound prunes almost nothing.
3. **DP is pseudo-polynomial.** With n fixed at 60, DP time grows in step with
   capacity (≈15 s at C ≈ 3.2 million) while B&B and the ILP stay under 0.1 s.
4. **The ILP is t
… [4605 more characters truncated]

# Track G — xv6 operating-system optimization
Two kernel features, each benchmarked on at least three workloads before and
after, on the same machine and the same QEMU configuration:
1. **A multilevel feedback queue (MLFQ) scheduler** to replace round robin
   (concept: process scheduling; objectives: response time and turnaround).
2. **Copy-on-write (COW) fork** to replace eager copying (concept: virtual
   memory and page faults; objectives: fork latency, memory use, pages copied).
**What to submit:** `patches/xv6-optimize.patch`, which is 1,257 lines
against upstream xv6-riscv commit `e93dabeb` (2026-10-26). `setup.sh`
re-creates the patched tree from a fresh clone. This was verified in the
session.

cd $HOME/td && python3 -c "
import os; os.environ['OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS']='1'
import numpy as np, time
rng=np.random.default_rng(0); X=rng.standard_normal((400000,50)); w=rng.standard_normal(50); y=X@w
for name,S in [('strided X[0::4]',X[0::4]),('contiguous block',X[:100000])]:
    t=time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(20): S.T@(S@w-y[:len(S)])
    print(name, round(time.perf_counter()-t,3))
t=time.perf_counter()
for _ in range(20): X.T@(X@w-y)
print('full serial',round(time.perf_counter()-t,3))
"

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Fix strided shards and rerun Exp 6 in the cloud</summary>

```
cd /home/claude/optimize-anything/tracks/D_submodular_parallel_distributed && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='distopt/parallel.py'; s=open(p).read()
old='''    i, procs, w = args
    X, y = _RESIDENT["X"][i::procs], _RESIDENT["y"][i::procs]'''
new='''    i, procs, w = args
    # contiguous row blocks: an earlier version took every procs-th row (X[i::procs]),
    # a strided view that made each matrix product ~9x slower (failure log F16)
    X, y = np.array_split(_RESIDENT["X"], procs)[i], np.array_split(_RESIDENT["y"], procs)[i]'''
assert old in s; s=s.replace(old,new)
old='''    shards = [(X[i::procs], y[i::procs], w) for i in range(procs)]'''
new='''    shards = [(Xs, ys, w) for Xs, ys in zip(np.array_split(X, procs), np.array_split(y, procs))]'''
assert old in s; s=s.replace(old,new)
open(p,'w').write(s)
EOF
python3 -m pytest -q -W ignore -p no:cacheprovider 2>&1 | tail -1; python3 -W ignore run_experiments.py --only 6 2>&1 | grep "parallel ("

tested on synthetic data and on seven real institutions from the Toronto

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Run ILP + hill climbing on hec-s-92</summary>

```
cat > hybrid_hec.py <<'EOF'
"""Hybrid for instances where every greedy construction fails (hec-s-92):
the min-slots ILP finds a clash-free timetable, then hill climbing lowers its
proximity cost. Five hill-climbing seeds, same as Experiment 1.

    python hybrid_hec.py [instance-name]
"""
import statistics as st
import sys
import time

from timetabling import data, evaluate, ilp, local_search

name = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "hec-s-92"
inst = data.load_carter(f"data/{name}.stu")
t0 = time.perf_counter()
r = ilp.min_slots(inst, time_limit=20)
t_ilp = time.perf_counter() - t0
assert evaluate.is_feasible(inst, r.slots)
print(f"{inst.summary()}")
print(f"min-slots ILP: {r.status}, {r.objective:.0f} slots, feasible, cost {evaluate.proximity_cost(inst, r.slots):.2f}, "
      f"{t_ilp:.1f} s")
costs, secs = [], []
for seed in range(5):
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    s = local_search.hill_climb(inst, r.slots, seed=seed)
    secs.append(time.perf_counter() - t0)
    assert evaluate.is_feasible(inst, s)
    costs.append(evaluate.proximity_cost(inst, s))
print(f"ILP + hill climb (5 seeds): cost {st.mean(costs):.2f} ± {st.pstdev(costs):.2f}, "
      f"min {min(costs):.2f}, {st.mean(secs):.2f} s per run")
EOF
timeout 300 python3 hybrid_hec.py | tee results/hybrid_hec.txt
```

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Add failure log entry F16</summary>

```
cd /home/claude/optimize-anything && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='docs/failure_log.md'; s=open(p).read()
entry='''## F16 · Track D · Strided data shards that undid data parallelism

- **Tool:** Claude (claude.ai, Claude Opus 5.5), 2026-11-01
- **What happened:** rerunning Track D on a 4-core VM on the student's
  laptop, the data-parallel gradient with data resident in the workers was
  slower than serial.
- **Cause:** the generated code gave worker i the rows `X[i::p]`. That is a
  strided NumPy view, so every matrix product walked memory with a stride of
  p rows. On that machine, 20 products over a strided quarter took 0.26 s,
  against 0.03 s for a contiguous quarter. On the 2-core cloud machine the
  effect was small enough to go unnoticed.
- **Fix:** contiguous row blocks (`np.array_split`).
- **Remaining effect:** even with the fix, the 4-core VM shows no gain for
  these memory-bound products. That is reported as a negative result, not
  hidden.
- **Lesson:** a locality problem (Exp 5 measures exactly this) hid inside
  the parallel code itself. Benchmarks on a second machine catch what one
  machine hides.

---

*(Add entries'''
s=s.replace("---\n\n*(Add entries",entry,1)
open(p,'w').write(s)
EOF
grep -c "^## F" docs/failure_log.md; sed -i 's/strided data shards that defeated data parallelism (F16)/strided data shards that defeated data parallelism (F16)/' report/REPORT.md
```

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Fetch the Toronto (Carter) exam-timetabling benchmark .stu files into data/.
# Source: the copy in github.com/pl-sch/its-exam-timetabling (data/toronto), pinned commit.
# Original: Carter, Laporte & Lee (1996); see https://people.cs.nott.ac.uk/pszrq/data.htm
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")"
COMMIT=d8eab9b7e91c13ae14b90dafcec588f605e0f67f
TMP=$(mktemp -d)
git clone -q https://github.com/pl-sch/its-exam-timetabling.git "$TMP/src"
git -C "$TMP/src" checkout -q "$COMMIT"
cp "$TMP"/src/data/toronto/*.stu data/
rm -rf "$TMP"
ls data/*.stu
echo "OK. Now: python run_experiments.py   (picks up every data/*.stu)"
EOF
chmod +x get_data.sh && python3 -c "
import csv
rows=list(csv.DictReader(open('results/exp2_ilp_vs_heuristic.csv')))
for r in rows: print(r['instance'],r['heuristic_cost'],r['ilp_warm_start'],r['ilp_cost'],r['ilp_lower_bound'])
"

<details><summary>Tool result</summary>

```
## 8. Known limitations

- **Single-exam moves only:** hill climbing uses one-exam moves. That is
  why it stays 30%+ above published Toronto results. Kempe-chain moves or
  simulated annealing are the next step.
- **hec-s-92 needs the ILP:** none of the greedy constructions is
  clash-free on it within its 18 slots.
- The proximity ILP has a very weak LP relaxation (fractional x can make every z
  zero), so its proven lower bound stays at 0 and the optimality gap cannot be
  closed within the time limit. It is useful as an improver of a heuristic
  solution, not as a proof of optimality.
- Hill climbing stops at the first local minimum; Kempe-chain moves or
  simulated annealing are natural next steps.
```

<li>Estimates mislead: planner costs, index sizes, simulated quantum speed-ups.</li>
<li>AI writes fast; independent checks make it correct.</li>

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Write and test a demo solve script</summary>

```
grep -n "^def " timetabling/greedy.py timetabling/local_search.py | head; cat > solve.py <<'EOF'
"""Demo: timetable one Toronto instance with the baseline and the optimized method.

    python solve.py data/yor-f-83.stu
"""
import sys
import time

from timetabling import data, evaluate, greedy, local_search

path = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "data/yor-f-83.stu"
inst = data.load_carter(path)
print(inst.summary())


def report(name, fn):
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    slots = fn()
    dt = time.perf_counter() - t0
    if slots is None or not evaluate.is_feasible(inst, slots):
        print(f"  {name:32s} no clash-free timetable in {inst.n_slots} slots  ({dt:.3f} s)")
        return None
    print(f"  {name:32s} proximity cost {evaluate.proximity_cost(inst, slots):8.2f}  ({dt:.3f} s)")
    return slots


report("first fit (baseline)", lambda: greedy.first_fit(inst))
report("DSatur", lambda: greedy.dsatur(inst))
start = report("DSatur-proximity", lambda: greedy.dsatur_proximity(inst))
if start is not None:
    report("+ hill climbing", lambda: local_search.hill_climb(inst, start, seed=0))
EOF
python3 solve.py data/yor-f-83.stu && python3 solve.py data/hec-s-92.stu
```

- **Write-up:** updated the README with the real-data results, comparing
  with the best results listed on the Nottingham benchmark page.

- First fit is clash-free on only 1 of 7 real institutions.
- hec-s-92 needs the ILP.
